Bogoliubov Theory for Trapped Bosons in the Gross-Pitaevskii Regime
Christian Brennecke, Benjamin Schlein, Severin SchravenInstitute for Applied Mathematics, University of Bonn, Endenicher Allee 60, 53115 Bonn, GermanyInstitute of Mathematics, University of Zurich, Winterthurerstrasse 190, 8057 Zurich, Switzerland

Abstract
We consider systems of N N bosons in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , trapped by an external potential. The interaction is repulsive and has a scattering length of the order N − 1 N^{-1} (Gross-Pitaevskii regime). We determine the ground state energy and the low-energy excitation spectrum up to errors that vanish in the limit N → ∞ N\to\infty .



1
Introduction and Main Results

We consider trapped bosons described by the Hamilton operator

H_{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left[-\Delta_{x_{j}}+V_{\text{ext}}(x_{j})\right]+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}N^{2}V(N(x_{i}-x_{j})),

(1.1)

acting on a dense subspace of
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
, the subspace of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
consisting of functions that are symmetric with respect to permutations of the
N
particles. Here,
V_{\text{ext}}\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
is a trapping potential with the property that
V_{\text{ext}}(x)\to\infty
for
|x|\to\infty
(later, we will formulate more precise assumptions on
V_{\text{ext}}
). As for the interaction
V
, we assume that
V\in L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and that it is pointwise non-negative, spherically symmetric and compactly supported.

The scattering length
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
of
V
is defined through the zero-energy scattering equation

\left[-\Delta+\frac{1}{2}V(x)\right]f(x)=0

(1.2)

with the boundary condition
f(x)\to 1
, as
|x|\to\infty
. For
|x|
large enough, we have

f(x)=1-\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}{|x|}

for a constant
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
which is determined by the potential
V
. This constant is called the scattering length of
V
. It is straightforward to show that

\displaystyle 8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)f(x)dx

and, by scaling, (
1.2
) implies that

\left[-\Delta+\frac{1}{2}N^{2}V(Nx)\right]f(Nx)=0.

This means that the scattering length of the interaction
N^{2}V(N.)
appearing in (
1.1
) is given by
\mathfrak{a}_{0}/N
, which characterises the Gross-Pitaeavskii regime.

From
[
20
,
19
,
22
]
it is well-known that the ground state energy
E_{N}
of the Hamilton operator (
1.1
) satisfies

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{E_{N}}{N}=\inf_{\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3}):\|\psi\|_{2}=1}\mathcal{E}_{GP}(\psi),

(1.3)

where
\mathcal{E}_{GP}
denotes the Gross-Pitaevskii functional, defined by

\mathcal{E}_{GP}(\psi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\left(|\nabla\psi(x)|^{2}+V_{\text{ext}}(x)|\psi(x)|^{2}+4\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}|\psi(x)|^{4}\right)dx.

(1.4)

We remark that
\mathcal{E}_{GP}
admits a unique normalized, strictly positive minimizer which we denote for the rest of this paper by
\varphi_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. It satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equation

\displaystyle-\Delta\varphi_{0}+V_{\text{ext}}\varphi_{0}+8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}|\varphi_{0}|^{2}\varphi_{0}=\varepsilon_{GP}\varphi_{0}

with the Lagrange multiplier
\varepsilon_{GP}=\mathcal{E}_{GP}(\varphi_{0})+4\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{4}^{4}
. In the Appendix, more precisely in Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
A.2
, we establish regularity and decay properties of the minimizer
\varphi_{0}
, which will be useful for our analysis.

From
[
18
,
19
,
22
]
, it is also known that every approximate ground state of (
1.1
), ie. every sequence of normalized wave functions
\psi_{N}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
satisfying

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\langle\psi_{N},H_{N}\psi_{N}\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{\text{GP}}(\varphi_{0})\,,

exhibits complete Bose-Einstein condensation in
\varphi_{0}
. In other words, denoting by
\gamma_{N}^{(1)}=\operatorname{tr}_{2,\dots,N}|\psi_{N}\rangle\langle\psi_{N}|
the one–particle reduced density associated with
a sequence of approximate ground states
\psi_{N}
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\langle\varphi_{0},\gamma_{N}^{(1)}\varphi_{0}\rangle=1.

(1.5)

For translation invariant systems (particles trapped in
\Lambda=[0;1]^{3}
with periodic boundary conditions), (
1.3
) and (
1.5
) have been proved in
[
3
,
6
,
1
,
16
]
to hold with the optimal rate of convergence. This result was generalized in
[
21
]
(extending the approach of
[
9
,
14
]
) to trapped systems described by (
1.1
), under the assumption of a sufficiently small scattering length
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. In
[
11
]
, we recently established (
1.3
) and (
1.5
) with optimal rate of convergence and with no assumption on the size of
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
. These bounds (obtained through a generalization of the methods of
[
3
,
6
]
) lead to the a-priori estimates stated below in Theorem
2.6
and Proposition
2.9
and represent a crucial ingredient for our analysis, aimed at understanding the low-energy spectrum of (
1.1
), in the limit
N\to\infty
.

To state our main theorem, we introduce the notation

H_{\text{GP}}=-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}+8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}-\varepsilon_{GP},\hskip 14.22636ptE=\big(H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}.

(1.6)

The operators
H_{\text{GP}}
and
E
have discrete spectrum and in both cases,
\varphi_{0}
is the unique, positive and normalized ground state vector with
H_{\text{GP}}\varphi_{0}=E\varphi_{0}=0
.

Let us make our assumptions on the interaction potential
V\in L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and the trapping potential
V_{\text{ext}}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
more precise. Our standing assumptions throughout the rest of this paper are as follows

\begin{split}(1)\;&V\in L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),V(x)\geq 0\text{ for }a.e.\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},V(x)=V(y)\text{ if }|x|=|y|,\\
&V\text{ compactly supported},\\
(2)\;&V_{\text{ext}}\in C^{1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{3};[0;\infty)\big),V_{\text{ext}}(x)\to\infty\text{ as }|x|\to\infty,\\
&\exists\;C>0\;\forall\;x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:V_{\text{ext}}(x+y)\leq C(V_{\text{ext}}(x)+C)(V_{\text{ext}}(y)+C),\\
&\nabla V_{\text{ext}}\text{ has at most exponential growth as }|x|\to\infty,\\
(3)\;&(H_{\text{GP}}+1)^{-3/4-\varepsilon}e^{-\alpha|x|}\text{ is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, for any $\varepsilon>0$}\\
&\text{and some $\alpha>0$}.\end{split}

(1.7)

It is worth mentioning that the assumption (2) implies that
V_{\text{ext}}
grows at most exponentially fast (see
[
11
, Appendix A]
for a proof). Combining this with the fact that
\varphi_{0}
decays faster than any exponential (see (
A.1
)) implies that we can control any
L^{p}
-norm of
V_{\text{ext}}\varphi_{0}
.

Our assumptions are technical and we have not attempted to optimize them. The assumption that
V_{\text{ext}}\geq 0
is for computational convenience only and can always be arranged by an energy shift (notice that we do not need to assume
V_{\text{ext}}\geq 0
in
[
11
]
).

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
V
and
V_{\text{ext}}
be as in (
1.7
). Then, there exists
\rho>0
such that, in the limit
N\to\infty
, the ground state energy
E_{N}
of
H_{N}
, defined in (
1.1
), is given by

\begin{split}E_{N}&=N\mathcal{E}_{GP}(\varphi_{0})-4\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{4}^{4}+E_{\text{Bog}}+\mathcal{O}\big(N^{-\rho}\big),\end{split}

(1.8)

where
E_{\text{Bog}}
a the finite constant (independent of
N
) given by

\begin{split}E_{\text{Bog}}=\frac{1}{2}\lim_{\delta\to 0}&\left\{\operatorname{tr}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}\Big[\Big(H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}1\!\!1_{\delta}\varphi_{0}^{2})H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}\Big)^{1/2}-H_{\text{GP}}-8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}1\!\!1_{\delta}\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big]\right.\\
&\hskip 156.49014pt\left.+\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}}{2}\operatorname{tr}\Big[\varphi_{0}^{2}1\!\!1_{\delta}\frac{1}{-\Delta}1\!\!1_{\delta}\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big]\right\}\end{split}

(1.9)

with
1\!\!1_{\delta}
denoting the approximation of the identity operator with integral kernel
1\!\!1_{\delta}(x;y)=(2\pi\delta)^{-3/2}e^{-(x-y)^{2}/2\delta^{2}}
(but any reasonable choice of the approximating sequence would do). Here,
\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
denotes the trace over the orthogonal complement
\{\varphi_{0}\}^{\bot}
.

Moreover, the spectrum of
H_{N}-E_{N}
below a threshold
\zeta>0
(assuming
\zeta\leq CN^{-\rho/5-\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0
) consists of eigenvalues of the form

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}n_{i}e_{i}+\mathcal{O}\big(N^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{5}+N^{-5\rho}\zeta^{30})\big),

(1.10)

where the
(e_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
denote the eigenvalues of the operator
E
, defined in (
1.6
), and where
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
with
n_{i}\neq 0
for finitely many
i\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark:
In (
1.9
), we cannot take directly the limit
\delta\to 0
, replacing
1\!\!1_{\delta}
with the identity, because the resulting operator is not of trace class and the trace would not be well-defined. Nevertheless, we will show in Section
3.3
that the limit defining
E_{\text{Bog}}
exists and that it is explicitly given by

\begin{split}E_{\emph{Bog}}&=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}\operatorname{tr}\left[\varphi_{0}^{2}(-\Delta+\kappa^{2})^{-1}(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi_{0}^{2}\right]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{4}(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}\left\|\varphi_{0}(-\Delta+\kappa^{2})^{-1}[\varphi_{0}^{2},-\Delta](-\Delta+\kappa^{2})^{-1/2}\right\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{4}(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}\left\|\varphi_{0}(-\Delta+\kappa^{2})^{-1}\nabla\varphi_{0}\right\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}}{4}\operatorname{tr}\,\Big[\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big(\frac{1}{-\Delta+\kappa^{2}}-\frac{1}{H_{\text{GP}}+\kappa^{2}}\Big)\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}}{4\kappa^{2}}\|\varphi_{0}^{3}\|^{2}+\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}}{4}\|(H_{\text{GP}}+\kappa^{2})^{-1/2}Q\varphi_{0}^{3}\|^{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}\kappa^{2}}{4}\operatorname{tr}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}\Big[\varphi_{0}^{2}Q\frac{1}{H_{\text{GP}}(H_{\text{GP}}+\kappa^{2})}Q\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{2}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\sqrt{s}\,\operatorname{tr}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}\frac{H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}}{s+H_{\text{GP}}^{2}}\Big[\Big[\varphi_{0}^{2},\frac{H_{\text{GP}}}{s+H_{\text{GP}}^{2}}\Big],\varphi_{0}^{2}\Big]\frac{H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}}{s+H_{\text{GP}}^{2}}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{4(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0})^{3}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{\infty}ds\,\sqrt{s}\operatorname{tr}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}\Big[\frac{1}{s+H_{\text{GP}}^{2}}H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}\varphi_{0}^{2}H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}\Big]^{3}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\times\frac{1}{s+H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}}\end{split}

(1.11)

for any
\kappa>0
large enough. In Sect.
3.3
we also prove that all contributions on the r.h.s. of (
1.11
) are finite.

Theorem
1.1
extends to the Gross-Pitaevskii regime earlier results obtained for systems of bosons in the mean-field limit. The low-energy spectrum of Hamilton operators describing mean-field bosons has been established in the translation invariant case in
[
25
,
12
]
and for systems trapped by external fields in
[
15
,
17
]
. More precise expansions in powers of
1/N
for the ground state energy and low energy excitations of mean field Hamiltonians has been recently obtained in
[
24
,
8
]
.

For translation invariant systems consisting of
N
bosons moving in the box
\Lambda=[0;1]^{3}
with periodic boundary conditions, Theorem
1.1
has been recently shown in
[
5
]
, using a rigorous version of Bogoliubov theory, see
[
7
]
. While the strategy that we are going to use to show Theorem
1.1
is similar to the one developed in
[
5
]
, the lack of translation invariance makes the proof more complicated.

Let us briefly explain the main steps. First of all, in Section
2
, we are going to factor out the Bose-Einstein condensate and restrict our attention on its orthogonal excitations. We do so through a unitary transformation
U_{N}
, first introduced in
[
17
]
, mapping the Hilbert space
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
onto the truncated Fock space
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}
constructed on the orthogonal complement of the condensate wave function
\varphi_{0}
. This procedure, which can be seen as a rigorous version of Bogoliubov c-number substitution, leads to the excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{L}_{N}=U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{*}
. In second quantized form, the operator
\mathcal{L}_{N}
can be written as the sum of a constant and of terms that are linear, quadratic, cubic and quartic in (modified) creation and annihilation operators. In contrast with the translation invariant case, where
\mathcal{L}_{N}
could be easily expressed in momentum space, here we have to use operator valued distributions, localized in position space.

It turns out that (similarly to the translation invariant case) cubic and quartic terms in the excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{L}_{N}
still contain important contributions to the ground state energy and to the energy of low-lying excited eigenvalues. To extract these contributions, we will conjugate
\mathcal{L}_{N}
with a (generalized) Bogoliubov transformation having the form
e^{B}
, with
B
quadratic in (modified) creation and annihilation operators.

Through the unitary operator
e^{B}
, we aim at removing short-scale correlations among particles; to reach this goal, we choose the integral kernel
\eta
defining
B
through the ground state solution of the Neumann problem associated with the interaction potential
N^{2}V(N.)
, in the ball of radius
\ell
, for a fixed
0<\ell<1
.

Conjugation with
e^{B}
leads to a new excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{G}_{N}=e^{-B}\mathcal{L}_{N}e^{B}
, with renormalized quadratic off-diagonal terms. The properties of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
are stated in Prop.
2.5
, which can be shown similarly as in the translation invariant setting. For completeness, we include a sketch of the proof, but we defer it to Section
4
.

Looking at the form of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
in Prop.
2.5
, we will observe that, while conjugation with
e^{B}
renormalizes quadratic terms, it does not change cubic terms. Similarly as in the translation invariant case, these terms cannot be neglected; they still contribute to the low-energy spectrum of the Hamilton operator. To extract the important contributions from the cubic terms, we conjugate
\mathcal{G}_{N}
with another unitary operator, this time given by the exponential
e^{A}
of a cubic expression in (modified) creation and annihilation operators.
This leads to a second renormalized excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{J}_{N}=e^{-A}\mathcal{G}_{N}e^{A}
, whose properties are stated in Prop
2.8
. As already observed in
[
26
]
(in a slightly different context) and then in
[
5
]
, it is important that scattering events described by
A
involve two particles with high momenta and one with small momentum (and one particle from the condensate). For technical reasons, we need to restrict to small momenta using a Gaussian, rather than a sharp cutoff (this guarantees that the cutoff decays sufficiently fast, also in position space). For this reason, the proof of Prop.
2.8
, given in Section
5
, is a bit more complicated than the proof of the corresponding result in the translation invariant setting (where sharp cutoffs could be used).

To control error terms produced by conjugation with
e^{B}
and
e^{A}
, we will make use of strong a-priori bounds stated in Theorem
2.6
and Proposition
2.9
; these important estimates follow from the proof of complete Bose-Einstein condensation obtained in
[
11
]
.

It follows from Prop.
2.8
that, up to negligible contributions, the renormalized excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{J}_{N}
is quadratic in creation and annihiliation operators (in fact,
\mathcal{J}_{N}
still contains a positive quartic term, which however can be neglected when proving lower bounds and which only produces small errors when evaluated on trial states that are appropriate to show upper bounds on
the low-energy eigenvalues). In Section
3
, we proceed therefore with the diagonalization of the quadratic part of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
. This is the main novelty of our analysis; the lack of translation invariant makes it much more involved than for particles on the torus. This is already clear by looking at the operators
H_{\text{GP}}
and
E
, defined in (
1.6
), which determine the ground state energy and the excited eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian. In the translation invariant case,
\varphi_{0}\equiv 1
and, in momentum space,
H_{\text{GP}}=p^{2}
and
E=(|p|^{4}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}p^{2})^{1/2}
. For systems trapped by external potentials, on the other hand,
\varphi_{0}
is not a constant and it does not commute with
H_{\text{GP}}
.

Finally, let us remark that while writing up the last details of our manuscript, a result similar to (
1.10
) was posted in
[
23
]
. While the strategy used in
[
23
]
is similar to ours (and to the one previously developed in
[
5
]
, in the translation invariant setting), the details of its implementation are different. Let us explain the main differences.

•

After removing the condensate through conjugation with the unitary map
U_{N}
, the authors of
[
23
]
extend the resulting excitation Hamiltonian, defined on the truncated Fock space with at most
N
particles, to the full Fock space. This step, which can be justified with a localization argument in the number of particles operator, allows them to work with standard creation and annihilation operators
a^{*},a
, rather than with the modified fields
b^{*},b
defined on
\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
(see (
2.4
) below), and simplifies a bit the analysis (notice however that the action of generalized Bogoliubov transformation, defined in terms of modified creation and annihilation operators,
b^{*},b
can be controlled quite well with bounds like those collected in Lemma
2.4
, which are, by now, standard tools; for this reason, working with
b^{*},b
and with generalized Bogoliubov transformations is, at least conceptually, not much more difficult than working with
a^{*},a
).

•

Another important difference between our work and
[
23
]
is the choice of the integral kernel
\eta
defining the (standard or generalized) Bogoliubov transformation and the cubic transformation renormalizing the excitation Hamiltonian. In our work,
\eta
is chosen through the ground state solution of the Neumann problem, defined on a ball of radius
\ell
, for a fixed
0<\ell<1
, independent of
N
. In
[
23
]
, on the other hand,
\eta
is defined through the solution of the zero-energy scattering equation (
1.2
), cutting off correlations at distance
1/N\ll\ell\ll 1
. While the choice of
\eta
in terms of the solution of the Neumann problem has the advantage that the cutoff at
|x|=\ell
does not contribute directly to the kinetic energy (in the sense that the radial derivative of
\eta
vanishes at
|x|=\ell
), the definition in terms of the zero-energy scattering equation allows for slightly more general interaction potentials (the result of
[
23
]
only requires
V\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
; in Theorem
1.1
, on the other hand, we assume
V\in L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, because this condition is needed to derive important properties of the solution of the Neumann problem). Also the different choice of
\ell
produces important differences between the two approaches. We choose
\ell
of order one, independent of
N
. This means that after quadratic and cubic renormalizations, the resulting excitation Hamiltonian only requires diagonalization on momenta of order one. In
[
23
]
, the authors choose
\ell=N^{-\alpha}
for some
0<\alpha<1
. The advantage of this choice is that the integral kernel
\eta
has small Hilbert-Schmidt norm; as a consequence, in a commutator expansion, terms of higher order in
\eta
are negligible. The price to pay for this choice of
\ell
is that, in the diagonalization, we need to act also on large momenta (the diagonalization, in this case, needs to renormalize also all momenta scales larger that one and smaller than
\ell^{-1}
). At the end, choosing
\ell
small, as done in
[
23
]
, seems to make the analysis slightly simpler.

•

While we and the authors of
[
23
]
both apply the same general strategy to diagonalize the renormalized excitation Hamiltonian, in our paper we make an additional effort to identify all non-vanishing contributions to the ground state energy
E_{N}
. In particular, our expression (
1.8
), with the Bogoliubov energy given by (
1.9
) or by (
1.11
), shows that the ground state energy, in the limit
N\to\infty
, only depends on the interaction potential through its scattering length
\mathfrak{a}_{0}
(notice that also the minimizer
\varphi_{0}
of the Gross-Pitaevskii energy functional only depends on
V
through its scattering length). In
[
23
]
, on the other hand, the ground state energy is left as a complicated expression depending on
N
and the limit is not further investigated (there is also no proof of the fact that
E_{N}-N\mathcal{E}_{\text{GP}}(\varphi_{0})
approaches a limit, as
N\to\infty
). In fact, it should be noted that our assumptions that
\nabla V_{\text{ext}}
grows at most exponentially and that
(H_{\text{GP}}+1)^{-3/4-\varepsilon}e^{-\alpha|x|}
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, for any
\varepsilon>0
and some
\alpha>0
, are only needed to study the behaviour of the ground state energy
E_{N}
, in the limit
N\to\infty
, and to establish the validity of (
1.8
), with
E_{\text{Bog}}
as in (
1.11
).

energetic properties of Bose-Einstein condensates”.

2
Excitation Hamiltonians

To prove Theorem
1.1
it is convenient to factor out the Bose-Einstein condensate and to focus instead on its orthogonal excitations. Recalling that
\varphi_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
denotes the unique normalized minimizer of the Gross-Pitaevskii functional (
1.4
), we consider the unitary map
U_{N}:L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})\to\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
mapping
L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
into the truncated Fock space

\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{N}L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes_{s}n}

constructed on the orthogonal complement
L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
of
\varphi_{0}
. The map
U_{N}
, first introduced in
[
17
]
, is defined by
U_{N}\psi_{N}=\{\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{N}\}
, if

\psi_{N}=\alpha_{0}\varphi_{0}^{\otimes N}+\alpha_{1}\otimes_{s}\varphi_{0}^{\otimes(N-1)}+\dots+\alpha_{N}

with
\alpha_{j}\in L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{\otimes j}
, for all
j=0,1,\dots,N
(here
\otimes_{s}
denotes the symmetric tensor product). The action of
U_{N}
on creation and annihilation operators is given by

\begin{split}U_{N}a^{*}(\varphi_{0})a(\varphi_{0})U_{N}^{*}&=N-\mathcal{N},\\
U_{N}a^{*}(f)a(\varphi_{0})U_{N}^{*}&=a^{*}(f)\sqrt{N-\mathcal{N}},\\
U_{N}a^{*}(\varphi_{0})a(g)U_{N}^{*}&=\sqrt{N-\mathcal{N}}a(g),\\
U_{N}a^{*}(f)a(g)U_{N}^{*}&=a^{*}(f)a(g)\end{split}

(2.1)

for all
f,g\in L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
\mathcal{N}
denotes the number of particles operator in
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
.

With
U_{N}
, we define the excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{L}_{N}=U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{*}
, acting on a dense subspace of
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
. Using the relations (
2.1
),
\mathcal{L}_{N}
can be written as

\mathcal{L}_{N}=\mathcal{L}^{(0)}_{N}+\mathcal{L}^{(1)}_{N}+\mathcal{L}^{(2)}_{N}+\mathcal{L}^{(3)}_{N}+\mathcal{L}^{(4)}_{N},

(2.2)

where, in the sense of forms in
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
, we have that

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{N}^{(0)}&=\big\langle\varphi_{0},\big[-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}+\frac{1}{2}\big(N^{3}V(N\cdot)*|\varphi_{0}|^{2}\big)\big]\varphi_{0}\big\rangle(N-\mathcal{N})\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\big\langle\varphi_{0},\big(N^{3}V(N\cdot)*|\varphi_{0}|^{2}\big)\varphi_{0}\big\rangle(\mathcal{N}+1)(1-\mathcal{N}/N),\\
\mathcal{L}_{N}^{(1)}&=\sqrt{N}b\left(\left(N^{3}V(N\cdot)*|\varphi_{0}|^{2}-8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}|\varphi_{0}|^{2}\right)\varphi_{0}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{\mathcal{N}+1}{\sqrt{N}}b\left(\left(N^{3}V(N\cdot)*|\varphi_{0}|^{2}\right)\varphi_{0}\right)+\text{h.c.},\\
\mathcal{L}_{N}^{(2)}&=\int dx\;a_{x}^{*}(-\Delta_{x})a_{x}+\int dx\;V_{\text{ext}}(x)a_{x}^{*}a_{x}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\int dxdy\;N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}^{2}(y)\Big(b_{x}^{*}b_{x}-\frac{1}{N}a_{x}^{*}a_{x}\Big)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\int dxdy\;N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\Big(b_{x}^{*}b_{y}-\frac{1}{N}a_{x}^{*}a_{y}\Big)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\;N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(y)\varphi_{0}(x)\Big(b_{x}^{*}b_{y}^{*}+b_{x}b_{y}\Big),\\
\mathcal{L}_{N}^{(3)}&=\int N^{5/2}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(y)\big(b_{x}^{*}a^{*}_{y}a_{x}+a^{*}_{x}a_{y}b_{x}\big)dxdy,\\
\mathcal{L}_{N}^{(4)}&=\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\;N^{2}V(N(x-y))a_{x}^{*}a_{y}^{*}a_{y}a_{x}.\end{split}

(2.3)

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we introduced here operator-valued distributions

b_{x}=\sqrt{\frac{N-\mathcal{N}}{N}}\,a_{x},\qquad b^{*}_{x}=a^{*}_{x}\,\sqrt{\frac{N-\mathcal{N}}{N}}

(2.4)

with
a_{x}^{*},a_{x}
denoting standard creation and annihilation operators. From the canonical commutation relations
[a_{x},a_{y}^{*}]=\delta(x-y)
,
[a_{x},b_{y}]=[a_{x}*,a_{y}^{*}]=0
, we obtain

[b_{x},b_{y}^{*}]=\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{N}}{N}\right)\delta(x-y)-\frac{1}{N}a_{y}^{*}a_{x},\hskip 28.45274pt[b_{x},b_{y}]=[b_{x}^{*},b_{y}^{*}]=0

(2.5)

and

\displaystyle[b_{x},a_{y}^{*}a_{z}]

\displaystyle=\delta(x-y)b_{z},\qquad[b_{x}^{*},a_{y}^{*}a_{z}]=-\delta(x-z)b_{y}^{*}.

For
f\in L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, it is also useful to define the operators

b(f)=\int\overline{f(x)}\,b_{x}\,dx=\sqrt{\frac{N-\mathcal{N}}{N}}a(f),\qquad b(f)=\int f(x)\,b^{*}_{x}\,dx=a^{*}(f)\sqrt{\frac{N-\mathcal{N}}{N}}.

Modified creation and annihilation operators can be controlled as the standard creation and annihilation operators, ie.

\begin{split}\|b(f)\xi\|\leq\|f\|\|\mathcal{N}^{1/2}\xi\|,\qquad\|b^{*}(f)\xi\|\leq\|f\|\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{1/2}\xi\|.\end{split}

where we recall that
\mathcal{N}
denotes the number of particles operator on
\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
.

To determine the spectrum of
\mathcal{L}_{N}
(and thus the spectrum of (
1.1
)), a naive application of Bogoliubov method would suggest to ignore all contributions except the constant and quadratic terms and to diagonalize the resulting Fock space Hamiltonian. This, however, does not yield the correct spectrum. In fact, it does not even produce the right leading order contribution to the ground state energy. The cubic and quartic terms in (
2.2
) contain relevant contributions to the energy. The point is that unitary conjugation with the map
U_{N}
(which leads to the excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{L}_{N}
) removes products of the condensate wave function, but it leaves correlations in the excitation vector
U_{N}\psi_{N}=\{\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{N}\}
. To extract the important contributions to the energy from cubic and quartic terms in (
2.3
), we need therefore to factor out some of the correlations among the particles. To this end, we follow the strategy introduced in
[
6
]
and we conjugate
\mathcal{L}_{N}
with a generalized Bogoliubov transformation.

To define the kernel of the Bogoliubov transformation, we consider the Neumann ground state
f_{\ell}
that solves

\left[-\Delta+\frac{1}{2}V\right]f_{\ell}=\lambda_{\ell}f_{\ell}

(2.6)

on the ball
|x|\leq N\ell
, for some fixed
0<\ell<1
. As explained below, we will choose the parameter
\ell>0
sufficiently small, but independent of
N
. To simplify notation, we do not indicate the
N
-dependence in
f_{\ell}
and
\lambda_{\ell}
. By radial symmetry of
f_{\ell}
, we can normalize it so that
f_{\ell}(x)=1
if
|x|=N\ell
. By scaling,
f_{\ell}(N.)
solves

\left[-\Delta+\frac{N^{2}}{2}V(Nx)\right]f_{\ell}(Nx)=N^{2}\lambda_{\ell}f_{\ell}(Nx)

on the ball where
|x|\leq\ell
. We then extend
f_{\ell}(N.)
to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, by setting
f_{N,\ell}(x)=f_{\ell}(Nx)
if
|x|\leq\ell
and
f_{N,\ell}(x)=1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
|x|>\ell
. Thus,
f_{N,\ell}
solves

\left(-\Delta+\frac{N^{2}}{2}V(N.)\right)f_{N,\ell}=N^{2}\lambda_{\ell}f_{N,\ell}\chi_{\ell},

(2.7)

where
\chi_{\ell}
denotes the characteristic function of the ball
B_{\ell}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Finally, we let
w_{\ell}=1-f_{\ell}
. Notice that
w_{\ell}(N.)
is support in
B_{\ell}(0)
. Defining the Fourier transform of
w_{\ell}
through

\widehat{w}_{\ell}(p)=\int dx\;w_{\ell}(x)e^{-2\pi ipx},

we obtain

\int dx\;w_{\ell}(Nx)e^{-2\pi ipx}=\frac{1}{N^{3}}\widehat{w}_{\ell}(p/N).

From (
2.7
), we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}-4\pi^{2}p^{2}\widehat{w}_{\ell}(p/N)+\frac{N^{2}}{2}(\widehat{V}(./N)\ast\widehat{f}_{N,\ell})(p)=N^{5}\lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{\chi}_{\ell}\ast\widehat{f}_{N,\ell})(p).\end{split}

The next lemma collects important properties of
f_{\ell},w_{\ell}
and the Neumann eigenvalue
\lambda_{\ell}
. Its proof is based on
[
13
, Lemma A.1]
,
[
6
, Lemma 4.1]
and can be found in
[
5
, Appendix B]
and
[
11
, Lemma 3.2]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
V\in L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
be non-negative, compactly supported and spherically symmetric. Fix
\ell>0
and let
f_{\ell}
denote the solution of (
2.6
).

i)

We have that

\displaystyle\lambda_{\ell}=\frac{3\mathfrak{a}_{0}}{(\ell N)^{3}}\left(1+\frac{9}{5}\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}{\ell N}+\mathcal{O}\big(\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{2}/(\ell N)^{2}\big)\right).

ii)

We have
0\leq f_{\ell},w_{\ell}\leq 1
and there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}V(x)f_{\ell}(x)dx-8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\Big(1+\frac{3}{2}\frac{\mathfrak{a}_{0}}{\ell N}\Big)\right|\leq\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{3}}{(\ell N)^{2}}

(2.8)

for all
\ell\in(0;1)
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
.

iii)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

w_{\ell}(x)\leq\frac{C}{|x|+1}\quad\text{ and }\quad|\nabla w_{\ell}(x)|\leq\frac{C}{|x|^{2}+1}

(2.9)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\ell\in(0;1)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough. Moreover,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{(N\ell)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}w_{\ell}(x)dx-\frac{2}{5}\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\Big|\leq\frac{C\mathfrak{a}_{0}^{2}}{N\ell}

for all
\ell\in(0;1)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough.

iv)

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

|\widehat{w}_{\ell}(p)|\leq\frac{C}{|p|^{2}}

(2.10)

for all
p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
\ell\in(0;1)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
large enough.

We define the kernel
k\in L_{s}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
through

k(x;y)=-Nw_{\ell}(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y).

(2.11)

With
Q=1-|\varphi_{0}\rangle\langle\varphi_{0}|
denoting the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of
\varphi_{0}
, we set

\eta(x;y)=\big[\big(Q\otimes Q\big)k\big](x;y).

(2.12)

The following lemma (whose proof can be found in
[
2
, Lemma 3.3]
,
[
10
, Lemma 4.3]
and in
[
11
, Lemma 2.2]
) summarizes important properties of the kernels
k,\eta
and
\mu=\eta-k
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Assume (
1.7
) and let
\ell\in(0;1)
. Then, there exists
C>0
, uniform in
N
,
\ell\in(0;1)
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, such that for
i=1,2
it holds true that

\begin{split}\|\eta\|&\leq C\ell^{1/2},\hskip 14.22636pt\|k\|\leq C\ell^{1/2},\hskip 14.22636pt\|\mu\|\leq C\ell^{1/2},\\
\|\eta_{x}\|&\leq C|\varphi_{0}(x)|,\hskip 14.22636pt\|k_{x}\|\leq C|\varphi_{0}(x)|,\hskip 14.22636pt\|\mu_{x}\|\leq C|\varphi_{0}(x)|,\\
\|\nabla_{i}\eta\|&\leq C\sqrt{N},\hskip 14.22636pt\,\|\nabla_{i}k\|\leq C\sqrt{N},\hskip 14.22636pt\|\nabla_{i}\mu\|\leq C,\hskip 14.22636pt\|\Delta_{i}\mu\|\leq C.\end{split}

(2.13)

Moreover, for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{0,1,2\}
we have the pointwise bounds

\begin{split}&|\eta(x;y)|,|k(x;y)|\leq C\frac{\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)}{|x-y|+N^{-1}},\,|\mu(x;y)|\leq C\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y),\\
&|\nabla_{1}\eta(x;y)|,|\nabla_{1}k(x;y)|\leq C\frac{\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)}{|x-y|^{2}+N^{-2}}+C\frac{|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|\varphi_{0}(y)}{|x-y|+N^{-1}},\\
&|\nabla_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\mu(x;y)|\leq C\sum_{j_{1}=0}^{\alpha_{1}}|\nabla^{j_{1}}\varphi_{0}(x)|\sum_{j_{2}=0}^{\alpha_{2}}|\nabla^{j_{2}}\varphi_{0}(y)|\end{split}

(2.14)

(and similarly for
\nabla_{2}\eta
,
\nabla_{2}k
). Finally, identifying the kernel
\eta
with the corresponding Hilbert-Schmidt operator and denoting by
\eta^{(n)}
the kernel of its
n
-th power, we find, for every
n\geq 2
and
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
that
\|\nabla_{i}\eta^{(n)}\|\leq C^{n}
,
\|\Delta_{i}\eta^{(n)}\|\leq C^{n}
and that

\begin{split}&|\eta^{(n)}(x;y)|\leq C\|\eta\|^{n-2}|\varphi_{0}(x)||\varphi_{0}(y)|,\\
&\|\nabla_{x}\eta^{(n)}_{x}\|\leq C(|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|+\varphi_{0}(x)),\\
&\|\Delta_{x}\eta^{(n)}_{x}\|\leq C(|\Delta\varphi_{0}(x)|+|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|+\varphi_{0}(x)).\end{split}

(2.15)

We introduce now the antisymmetric operator

B=\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,\big[\eta(x;y)b_{x}^{*}b_{y}^{*}-\text{h.c.}\big]

(2.16)

and we consider the generalized Bogoliubov transformation
e^{B}
. Since
\eta\in(Q\otimes Q)L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the unitary operator
e^{B}
maps the Hilbert space
\mathcal{F}^{\leq N}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}
back into itself. Another important property of
e^{B}
is the fact that it preserves, approximately, the number of particles (ie. the number of excitations of the Bose-Einstein condensate). The following lemma was proved in
[
10
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\eta\in L_{s}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
B
be the antisymmetric operator defined in (
2.16
). For every
n\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C>0
(depending only on
n
and on
\|\eta\|
) such that

e^{-B}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n}e^{B}\leq C(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n}

as an operator inequality on
\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
.

We will also need more precise information on the action of the
e^{B}
. Keeping in mind the fact that, if we replaced in (
2.16
) the
b^{*},b
-operators with standard creation and annihilation operators,
e^{B}

would be a Bogoliubov transformation with explicit action, we decompose, for an arbitrary
f\in L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

e^{-B}b(f)e^{B}=b(\cosh_{\eta}(f))+b^{*}(\sinh_{\eta}(\overline{f}))+d_{\eta}(f),

(2.17)

where

\begin{split}\cosh_{\eta}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{(2n)}}{(2n)!},\hskip 14.22636pt\sinh_{\eta}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\eta^{(2n+1)}}{(2n+1)!}.\end{split}

(2.18)

We also introduce the operator-valued distributions
d_{\eta,x},d^{*}_{\eta,x}
defined through

e^{-B}b_{x}e^{B}=b(\cosh_{\eta,x})+b^{*}(\sinh_{\eta,x})+d_{\eta,x}.

(2.19)

We will use the short-hand notations

\gamma=\cosh_{\eta},\qquad\sigma=\sinh_{\eta},\qquad\text{p}_{\eta}=\gamma-1,\qquad\text{r}_{\eta}=\sigma-\eta.

(2.20)

It is a simple consequence of Lemma
2.2
that, under the same assumptions as in Lemma
2.2
, there is a constant
C>0
such that, for all
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\{0,1,2\}
with
\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\leq 2
, we have

\|\nabla_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\emph{p}_{\eta}\|,\|\nabla_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\nabla_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\emph{r}_{\eta}\|\leq C.

Moreover, we have the bounds
|\emph{p}(x;y)|,|\emph{r}(x;y)|\leq C\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The idea that on states with few excitations
e^{B}
acts almost as a Bogoliubov transformation is confirmed by the next lemma, which bounds the norm of the operators
d_{\eta}(f)
, in terms of (appropriate powers of) the number of particles operator. This lemma, whose proof is an adaptation of the translation-invariant case
[
6
, Lemma 3.4]
, requires
\|\eta\|
to be sufficiently small. From Lemma
2.2
, this can be achieved requiring that
\ell>0
is small enough.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Let
\eta\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
be as defined in (
2.16
), with
\ell>0
small enough. Let
d_{\eta}(f)
be as in (
2.17
) (and
d_{\eta,x}
as defined in (
2.19
)). Then there exists
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n/2}d_{\eta}(f)\xi\|&\leq\frac{C}{N}\|f\|\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+3)/2}\xi\|,\\
\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n/2}d_{\eta}(f)^{*}\xi\|&\leq\frac{C}{N}\|f\|\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+3)/2}\xi\|\end{split}

and such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have

\begin{split}\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n/2}d_{\eta,x}\xi\|&\leq\frac{C}{N}\Big[\|a_{x}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+2)/2}\xi\|+\varphi_{0}(x)\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+3)/2}\xi\|\Big].\end{split}

(2.21)

Furthermore, if we set
\overline{d}_{\eta,x}=d_{\eta,x}+(\mathcal{N}/N)b^{*}(\eta_{x})
, we find

\begin{split}&\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n/2}a_{y}\overline{d}_{\eta,x}\xi\|\\
&\leq\frac{C}{N}\Big[\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+2)/2}\xi\|+|\eta(x;y)|\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+2)/2}\xi\|\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\varphi_{0}(y)\|a_{x}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+1)/2}\xi\|+\varphi_{0}(x)\|a_{y}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+3)/2}\xi\|\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\|a_{x}a_{y}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+2)/2}\xi\|\Big]\end{split}

(2.22)

and, finally, we have that

\begin{split}&\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{n/2}d_{\eta,x}d_{\eta,y}\xi\|\\
&\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\Big[\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+6)/2}\xi\|+|\eta(x;y)|\|(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+4)/2}\xi\|\\
&\hskip 34.14322pt+\varphi_{0}(y)\|a_{x}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+5)/2)}\xi\|+\varphi_{0}(x)\|a_{y}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+5)/2}\xi\|\\
&\hskip 34.14322pt+\|a_{x}a_{y}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{(n+4)/2}\xi\|\Big].\end{split}

(2.23)

Using the generalized Bogoliubov transformation
e^{B}
, we define the renormalized excitation Hamiltonian

\mathcal{G}_{N}=e^{-B}\mathcal{L}_{N}e^{B}.

(2.24)

In the next proposition, we collect some important properties of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
. Here and in the rest of the paper,
we are going to use the notation

\begin{split}\mathcal{K}&=\int dx\;a^{*}_{x}(-\Delta_{x})a_{x},\hskip 99.58464pt\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}}=\int dx\,V_{\text{ext}}(x)a^{*}_{x}a_{x},\\
\mathcal{V}_{N}&=\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\;N^{2}V(N(x-y))a^{*}_{x}a^{*}_{y}a_{y}a_{x},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{H}_{N}=\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}}+\mathcal{V}_{N}.\end{split}

Proposition 2.5
.

Assume (
1.7
), let
\eta
be defined as in (
2.12
) and let
E_{N}
denote the ground state energy of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
. Then the following holds true:

a)

We have that
|E_{N}-N\mathcal{E}_{GP}(\varphi_{0})|\leq C
and

\mathcal{G}_{N}-E_{N}=\mathcal{H}_{N}+\Delta_{N},

(2.25)

where the error term
\Delta_{N}
is such that for every
\delta>0
, there exists
C>0
with

\pm\Delta_{N}\leq\delta\mathcal{H}_{N}+C(\mathcal{N}+1).

(2.26)

Furthermore, for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a
C>0
such that

\pm\text{ad}^{\,(k)}_{\,i\mathcal{N}}(\mathcal{G}_{N})=\pm\text{ad}^{\,(k)}_{\,i\mathcal{N}}(\Delta_{N})=\pm\big[i\mathcal{N},\dots\big[i\mathcal{N},\Delta_{N}\big]\dots\big]\leq C(\mathcal{H}_{N}+1).

(2.27)

b)

Let
\sigma
and
\gamma
be defined as in (
2.18
) and let
\kappa_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
denote the constant

\begin{split}\kappa_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}&=N\big\langle\varphi_{0},(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}+\widehat{V}(0)\varphi_{0}^{2}/2)\varphi_{0}\big\rangle-4\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{4}^{4}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\operatorname{tr}\big(\sigma(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP})\sigma\big)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\operatorname{tr}\big(\gamma\big[N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big]\sigma)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\operatorname{tr}\big(\sigma\big[N^{3}V(N.)\ast\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big]\sigma\big)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,N^{2}V(N(x-y))|\langle\sigma_{x},\gamma_{y}\rangle|^{2}.\end{split}

(2.28)

Here,
(N^{3}V(N.)\ast\varphi_{0}^{2})
acts as multiplication operator and we identify kernels like
N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
with their associated Hilbert Schmidt operators. Let

\begin{split}\Phi&=\gamma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\gamma+\sigma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\sigma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\gamma\big(N^{3}V(N.)\ast\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\sigma\big(N^{3}V(N.)\ast\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\sigma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\Big(\gamma\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\sigma+\emph{h.c.}\Big),\end{split}

(2.29)

and

\begin{split}\Gamma&=\gamma\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sigma\!\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\!\sigma\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\big[\sigma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\gamma+\emph{h.c.}\big]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\big[\sigma\big(N^{3}V(N.)\ast\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma+\emph{h.c.}\big].\\
\end{split}

(2.30)

With
\Phi
and
\Gamma
, we then define the quadratic Fock space Hamiltonian

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}=\int dxdy\,\Phi(x;y)b^{*}_{x}b_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,\Gamma(x;y)\big(b^{*}_{x}b^{*}_{y}+b_{x}b_{y}\big),\end{split}

(2.31)

and we denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
the cubic operator

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}=\int dxdy\,N^{5/2}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(y)b^{*}_{x}b^{*}_{y}\big(b(\gamma_{x})+b^{*}(\sigma_{x})\big)+\emph{h.c.}

(2.32)

Then, we have that

\begin{split}\mathcal{G}_{N}&=\kappa_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}+\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}+\mathcal{V}_{N}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}\end{split}

(2.33)

for a self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
that satisfies in
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
the operator bound

\pm\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}\leq CN^{-1/2}(\mathcal{H}_{N}+\mathcal{N}^{2}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1).

(2.34)

The proof of Prop.
2.5
is similar to the proof of
[
5
, Prop. 3.2]
. For completeness, we sketch it in Sect.
4
.

Combining Prop.
2.5
with the lower bound

\mathcal{G}_{N}-E_{N}\geq c\mathcal{N}-C

(2.35)

from
[
11
, Eq. (3.1)]
(and from Lemma
2.3
in the present paper), we obtain strong a-priori estimates on the number and on the energy of excitations of the Bose-Einstein condensate in low-energy states of (
1.1
). These bounds are crucial for the rest of out analysis.

Theorem 2.6
.

Assume (
1.7
), let
\eta
be defined as in (
2.12
) and let
E_{N}
denote the ground state energy of
H_{N}
, defined in (
1.1
). Let
\psi_{N}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
with
\|\psi_{N}\|=1
belong to the spectral subspace of
H_{N}
with energies below
E_{N}+\zeta
, for some
\zeta>0
, i.e.

\psi_{N}={\bf 1}_{(-\infty;E_{N}+\zeta]}(H_{N})\psi_{N}.

Let
\xi_{N}=e^{-B}U_{N}\psi_{N}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
be the renormalized excitation vector associated with
\psi_{N}
. Then, for every
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\langle\xi_{N},(\mathcal{H}_{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\xi_{N}\rangle\leq C(1+\zeta^{j+1}).

Proof.

The proof carries over from
[
5
, Prop. 4.1]
, once we show that

\mathcal{G}_{N}-E_{N}\geq c\,\mathcal{H}_{N}-C

(2.36)

for two positive constants
c,C>0
, independent of
N
. Indeed, this enables us to use the induction argument from
[
5
, Prop. 4.1]
combined with the bounds (
2.27
). The bound (
2.36
) can be shown interpolating (
2.35
) with the estimate

\mathcal{G}_{N}-E_{N}\geq\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}_{N}-C(\mathcal{N}+1)

which follows from (
2.25
) and (
2.26
).
∎

Although Prop.
2.5
and Theorem
2.6
provide strong control on low-energy states of
\mathcal{G}_{N}
, this is not enough to deduce Theorem
1.1
. The reason is that the cubic operator
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
and the quartic operator
\mathcal{V}_{N}
appearing on the r.h.s. of (
2.32
) still contain energy contributions of order one. To extract them, we follow again the strategy introduced in
[
6
]
and we conjugate
\mathcal{G}_{N}
with an additional unitary transformation, this time generated by an operator cubic in the modified creation and annihilation fields.

In order to define our cubic transformation, we need to introduce some notation. For an exponent
\varepsilon>0
to be specified later on, we use
\chi_{H}(p)=\chi(|p|>N^{\varepsilon})
to restrict to high momenta
|p|>N^{\varepsilon}
. We consider the kernel

\widetilde{k}_{H}(x;y)=\left[-Nw_{\ell}(N.)*\check{\chi}_{H}\right](x-y)\varphi_{0}(y).

(2.37)

On the other hand, to restrict to small momenta, we use a Gaussian cutoff. For
0<\tau<\varepsilon
, we set
g_{L}(p)=e^{-p^{2}/N^{2\tau}}
. The advantage of
g_{L}
, with respect to a sharp cutoff, is that it decays fast also in position space. In particular, from the assumptions (
1.7
) on
V_{\text{ext}}
, we find

|V_{\text{ext}}*\check{g}_{L}|\leq C(V_{\text{ext}}+1).

(2.38)

Recalling the definition of
\sigma,\gamma
in (
2.20
), we introduce the notation

\sigma_{L}=\sigma*_{2}\check{g}_{L},\qquad\gamma_{L}=\gamma*_{2}\check{g}_{L}

(2.39)

where
*_{2}
is the convolution in the second variable. In particular, for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have
\sigma_{L,x}=\sigma_{x}*\check{g}_{L}
and
\gamma_{L,x}=\gamma_{x}*\check{g}_{L}
.

In the next lemma, we collect useful bounds for various kernels.

Lemma 2.7
.

We assume that
0<3\tau\leq\varepsilon
. Let
\widetilde{k}_{H},\sigma_{L}
and
\gamma_{L}
as in (
2.37
) and (
2.39
). Then we have

\begin{split}&\|\widetilde{k}_{H}\|\leq CN^{-\varepsilon/2},\quad\sup_{x}\|\widetilde{k}_{H}(x,.)\|\leq CN^{-\varepsilon/2},\quad\|\widetilde{k}_{H}(.,y)\|\leq CN^{-\varepsilon/2}\varphi_{0}(y)\end{split}

(2.40)

for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Setting
\widetilde{k}(x;y)=-Nw_{\ell}(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(y)
(no cutoff), we have

\|\nabla_{x}(\widetilde{k}-\widetilde{k}_{H})(x,.)\|\leq CN^{\varepsilon/2},\qquad|(\widetilde{k}-\widetilde{k}_{H})(x;y)|\leq CN^{\varepsilon}\varphi_{0}(y).

(2.41)

Moreover, we find

\begin{split}\|\sigma_{L,x}\|,\|\big[(\gamma-1)*_{2}\check{g}_{L}\big]_{x}\|\leq C\varphi_{0}(x),\end{split}

(2.42)

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which implies
\|\sigma_{L}\|,\|(\gamma-1)*_{2}\check{g}_{L}\|,\|\gamma_{L}\|_{\text{op}}\leq C
, and also

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|V_{\text{ext}}\big[(\gamma-1)*_{2}\check{g}_{L}\big]\|,\|V_{\text{ext}}\sigma_{L}\|\leq C.\end{split}

Finally, we have

\|\nabla_{x}\sigma_{L,x}\|\leq CN^{\tau/2}\big[\varphi_{0}(x)+|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|\big],\quad\|\nabla_{x}\big[(\gamma-1)_{x}*_{2}\check{g}_{L}\big]\|\leq C\big[\varphi_{0}(x)+|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|\big].

(2.43)

Proof.

Using (
2.10
) we compute

\displaystyle\|Nw_{\ell}(N.)*\check{\chi}_{H}\|^{2}\leq C\int_{|p|\geq N^{\varepsilon}}|p|^{-4}dp=CN^{-\varepsilon}.

This implies all the bounds in (
2.40
). To show (
2.41
), we observe that
(\widetilde{k}-\widetilde{k}_{H})(x;y)=\big[-Nw_{\ell}(N.)*\check{\chi}_{H^{c}}\big](x-y)\varphi_{0}(y)
, with
\chi_{H_{c}}
the indicator function of the set
\{p\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:|p|\leq N^{\varepsilon}\}
and that, from (
2.10
),

\big\|\nabla\big[-Nw_{\ell}(N.)*\check{\chi}_{H^{c}}\big]\big\|^{2}_{2}=\int_{|p|\leq N^{\varepsilon}}dp\left[\frac{|p|}{N^{2}}|\hat{w}_{\ell}(p/N)|\right]^{2}\leq CN^{\varepsilon}

and

\|-Nw_{\ell}(N.)*\check{\chi}_{H^{c}}\|_{\infty}\leq C\int_{|p|\leq N^{\varepsilon}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\hat{w}_{\ell}(p/N)dp\leq CN^{\varepsilon}.

To prove (
2.42
), we estimate, with (
2.13
), (
2.15
), Parseval’s identity and
|g_{L}|\leq 1
,

\|\sigma_{L,x}\|=\|\widehat{\sigma_{x}}g_{L}\|\leq\|\sigma_{x}\|\leq\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{1}{(2n+1)!}\|\eta^{(2n+1)}_{x}\|\leq C\varphi_{0}(x).

The bound for
(\gamma-1)*_{2}\check{g}_{L}
can be proven similarly. Finally, we show (
2.43
). With (
2.15
), it is easy to check that
\|\nabla_{x}(\sigma_{L}-k*_{2}\check{g}_{L})_{x}\|\leq C(\varphi_{0}(x)+|\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)|)
, with
k
as defined in (
2.11
). We have

\begin{split}\nabla_{x}(k*_{2}\check{g}_{L})(x;y)=&-\int N^{2}\nabla w_{\ell}(N(x-z-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y+z)\check{g}_{L}(z)dz\\
&-\int Nw_{\ell}(N(x-z-y))\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y+z)\check{g}_{L}(z)dz.\end{split}

(2.44)

The
L^{2}
-norm squared (in the variable
y
, for fixed
x
) of the first term can be bounded, with (
2.9
), by

\begin{split}\int dy&dz_{1}dz_{2}\frac{1}{|x-z_{1}-y|^{2}|x-z_{2}-y|^{2}}\varphi_{0}(x)^{2}\varphi_{0}(y+z_{1})\varphi_{0}(y+z_{2})\check{g}_{L}(z_{1})\check{g}_{L}(z_{2})\\
\leq\;&C\int dz_{1}dz_{2}\,\frac{1}{|z_{1}-z_{2}|}\check{g}_{L}(z_{1})\check{g}_{L}(z_{2})\leq CN^{\tau}\int dw_{1}dw_{2}\frac{1}{|w_{1}-w_{2}|}e^{-w_{1}^{2}}e^{-w_{2}^{2}}\leq CN^{\tau}\end{split}

where we switched to new variables
w_{i}=z_{i}N^{\tau}
, for
i=1,2
. The second term on the r.h.s. of (
2.44
) can be controlled analogously.
∎

We introduce the antisymmetric operator

A=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\int dxdy\,\widetilde{k}_{H}(x;y)\widetilde{b}_{x}^{*}\widetilde{b}_{y}^{*}\left[\widetilde{b}(\gamma_{L,x})+\widetilde{b}^{*}(\sigma_{L,x})\right]-\text{h.c.}

(2.45)

In order to preserve the space
\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
and stay orthogonal to
\varphi_{0}
, we used here
the operators

\widetilde{b}_{x}=b(Q_{x})=\int dz\,Q(x;z)b_{z}

with
Q=1-|\varphi_{0}\rangle\langle\varphi_{0}|
. Using
A
, we define the cubically renormalized excitation Hamiltonian
\mathcal{J}_{N}=e^{-A}\mathcal{G}_{N}e^{A}
.

The next proposition summarises important properties of the operator
\mathcal{J}_{N}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Assume (
1.7
) and let
0<6\tau\leq\varepsilon\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then we have

\mathcal{J}_{N}=\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\mathcal{V}_{N}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}},

(2.46)

where

\begin{split}\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}&=\kappa_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}-\emph{tr}(\sigma N^{3}(Vw_{\ell})(N.)*\varphi_{0}^{2}\sigma)-\emph{tr}(\sigma N^{3}(Vw_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\sigma)\end{split}

(2.47)

with
\kappa_{\mathcal{G}_{N}}
defined in (
2.28
) and where the quadratic operator
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}=\int dxdy\,\widetilde{\Phi}(x;y)b^{*}_{x}b_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\,\widetilde{\Gamma}(x;y)\big(b^{*}_{x}b^{*}_{y}+b_{x}b_{y}\big)\end{split}

(2.48)

for

\begin{split}\widetilde{\Phi}&=\gamma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\gamma+\sigma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\sigma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\gamma\big(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\sigma\big(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\sigma\\
&\hskip 11.38092pt+\Big(\gamma\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\sigma+\emph{h.c.}\Big),\end{split}

(2.49)

and

\begin{split}\widetilde{\Gamma}&=\gamma\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sigma\!\big(N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\!\sigma\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\big[\sigma\big(-\Delta+V_{\text{ext}}-\varepsilon_{GP}\big)\gamma+\emph{h.c.}\big]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\big[\sigma\big(8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}+N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\gamma+\emph{h.c.}\big].\\
\end{split}

(2.50)

Moreover, the self-adjoint operator
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
is bounded by

\pm\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\leq\frac{C}{N^{\min\{\frac{\tau}{2},\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\}}}(\mathcal{H}_{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3}.

(2.51)

The proof of Prop.
2.8
, which is similar to the proof of
[
5
, Prop. 3.3]
, is sketched it in Sect.
4
.

In order to compute the spectrum of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
, we need to have good control on the number of excitations of wave functions in low-energy spectral subspaces of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
. This is summarized in the following proposition whose proof is deferred to Section
5
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Assume (
1.7
), let
\eta
be defined as in (
2.12
),
A
as defined in (
2.45
) and let
E_{N}
denote the ground state energy of
H_{N}
. Let
\psi_{N}\in L^{2}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3N})
with
\|\psi_{N}\|=1
belong to the spectral subspace of
H_{N}
with energies below
E_{N}+\zeta
, for some
\zeta>0
, i.e.

\psi_{N}={\bf 1}_{(-\infty;E_{N}+\zeta]}(H_{N})\psi_{N}.

Let
\xi_{N}=e^{-A}e^{-B}U_{N}\psi_{N}\in\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
be the renormalized excitation vector associated with
\psi_{N}
. Then, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\langle\xi_{N},(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}(\mathcal{H}_{N}+1)\xi_{N}\rangle\leq C(1+\zeta^{j+3}).

3
Bounds on
\sigma(H_{N})
and Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section, we conclude the proof of Theorem
1.1
and determine the ground state energy
E_{N}
of
H_{N}
as well as the spectrum of
H_{N}-E_{N}
below a threshold
\zeta>0
, up to errors that vanish in the limit
N\to\infty
. Our proof is based on Proposition
2.8
, recalling that
\mathcal{J}_{N}=e^{-A}e^{-B}U_{N}H_{N}U_{N}^{*}e^{B}e^{A}
is unitarily equivalent to
H_{N}
. For the rest of this section, we assume that the parameter
\ell\in(0;1)
introduced in (
2.6
) is sufficiently small (by Lemma
2.2
, this guarantees that
\|\eta\|
is small enough). We also assume that the parameters introduced around
(
2.37
) are fixed as
\varepsilon=6/13
and
\tau=\varepsilon/6
, so that we can apply the results of Proposition
2.5
and Proposition
2.8
. From (
2.51
), we have

\pm\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\leq CN^{-\rho}(\mathcal{H}_{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{3},

(3.1)

with
\rho=1/26
.

To compute the spectrum of
H_{N}
, we proceed in two main steps, proving first lower and then upper bounds.
To show the lower bound, we start from (
2.46
), we drop the non-negative potential energy
\mathcal{V}_{N}
in (
2.46
) and then we diagonalize the remaining quadratic Fock space Hamiltonian. This is discussed in the Section
3.1
. For the upper bound, we need additionally to control the expectation of the potential energy on low energy states; this is discussed in Section
3.2
. Combining the two results, we obtain asymptotically matching lower and upper bounds on the min-max values of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
. To conclude Theorem
1.1
, it then only remains to verify Eq. (
1.8
) which determines the ground state energy
E_{N}
up to errors that vanish as
N\to\infty
. This is the content of Section
3.3
.

Before we start with the lower bound, it is convenient to switch to the full excitation Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
, replacing the modified creation and annihilation operators in (
2.48
) by the standard ones. This will enable us to diagonalize the resulting quadratic Fock space Hamiltonian exactly. To this end, let us denote by

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}=\int dxdy\,\widetilde{\Phi}(x,y)a_{x}^{*}a_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\int dxdy\ \widetilde{\Gamma}(x,y)(a_{x}^{*}a_{y}^{*}+\text{h.c.})

(3.2)

the quadratic Fock space Hamiltonian that is obtained from
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
replacing
b,b^{*}
operators by the
a,a^{*}
fields. We claim that

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{N}

(3.3)

for an error
\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
that is bounded, in the sense of forms in
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
, by

\pm\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{N}\leq CN^{-1/2}(\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}}+\mathcal{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1).

(3.4)

To prove the bound (
3.4
), consider first the non-diagonal contribution to
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
. From the definition (
2.4
) and using that
|\sqrt{1-x/N}\sqrt{1-x/N-1/N}-1|\leq Cx/N
for
0\leq x\leq N
, we bound

\pm\bigg(\int dxdy\ \widetilde{\Gamma}(x,y)(b_{x}^{*}b_{y}^{*}-a_{x}^{*}a_{y}^{*}+\text{h.c.})\bigg)\leq CN^{-1}\|\widetilde{\Gamma}\|_{\text{HS}}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{2}.

(3.5)

In particular, this error is small if we show that

\|\widetilde{\Gamma}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C

(3.6)

for some constant
C>0
, independent of
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Here, we identify the kernel
\widetilde{\Gamma}
with the corresponding operator on
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\|\cdot\|_{\text{HS}}
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm. To control
\|\widetilde{\Gamma}\|_{\text{HS}}
, we consider the different contributions to
\widetilde{\Gamma}
in (
2.50
). We first note that the operator with kernel
N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
is bounded, uniformly in
N
. Analogously,
\|8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}\|_{op}\leq C
. From Lemma
2.2
, we have
\|\sigma\|_{\text{HS}}<\infty
, and also
\|\sigma V_{\text{ext}}\|_{\text{HS}},\|\sigma\Delta\gamma-k\Delta\|_{\text{HS}}<\infty
. Thus, to show (
3.6
), we only need to control the norm of the operator on
L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with kernel

\begin{split}N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))&\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)+(\Delta_{x}+\Delta_{y})\big(Nw_{\ell}(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)\\
=\;&N^{3}\left[2\Delta w_{\ell}(N(x-y))+(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\right]\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\\
&+Nw_{\ell}(N(x-y))\big[\Delta\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)+\varphi_{0}(x)\Delta\varphi_{0}(y)\big]\\
&+2N^{2}\nabla w_{\ell}(N(x-y))\cdot\big[\nabla\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)-\varphi_{0}(x)\nabla\varphi_{0}(y)\big].\end{split}

(3.7)

With the scattering equation (
2.6
) (and using the bounds in Lemma
2.1
), we can show that the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of the operator associated with the kernel on the first line is bounded, uniformly in
N
. The operator associated with the kernel on the second line is also Hilbert-Schmidt, with norm bounded uniformly in
N
. As for the kernel on the last line, we can write

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}\varphi_{0}(x)&\varphi_{0}(y)-\varphi_{0}(x)\nabla_{i}\varphi_{0}(y)\\
&=\int_{0}^{1}ds\,\frac{d}{ds}\,\nabla_{i}\varphi_{0}(sx+(1-s)y)\varphi_{0}((1-s)x+sy)\\
&=\sum_{j=1}^{3}(x_{j}-y_{j})\int_{0}^{1}ds\,\Big[\big(\nabla_{i}\nabla_{j}\varphi_{0}\big)(sx+(1-s)y)\varphi_{0}((1-s)x+sy)\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+\big(\nabla_{i}\varphi_{0}\big)(sx+(1-s)y)\big(\nabla_{j}\varphi_{0}\big)((1-s)x+sy)\Big].\end{split}

With the factor
(x-y)
multiplying
N^{2}(\nabla w_{\ell})(N(x-y))
, and with the estimates (
2.9
), (
A.2
), we conclude that also the last term on the right hand side of (
3.7
) has a bounded Hilbert-Schmidt norm. This concludes the proof of (
3.6
) and shows that the error (
3.5
) is small. Similarly, we can also bound the diagonal part of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
. We find

\pm\bigg(\int dxdy\ \widetilde{\Phi}(x,y)(b_{x}^{*}b_{y}-a_{x}^{*}a_{y})\bigg)\leq CN^{-1}(\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}}+\mathcal{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1).

which completes the proof of (
3.4
).

3.1
Lower Bound on
\sigma(H_{N})

In this section, we compare the min-max values of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
with those of the quadratic operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
, defined through (
3.2
) as a quadratic form in the full excitation Fock space
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
. To obtain a lower bound on the spectrum of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
, suppose that
\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})-E_{N}\leq\zeta
. Then the min-max principle implies with (
2.46
), (
3.1
), (
3.3
), (
3.4
) and Proposition
2.9

\begin{split}\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})&=\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\,\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\subset P_{\zeta}(\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}),\\
\dim(Y)=n\end{subarray}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in Y:\\
\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\langle\xi,(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\mathcal{V}_{N}+\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}_{N})\xi\rangle\\
&\geq\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}Y\subset P_{\zeta}(\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}),\\
\dim(Y)=n\end{subarray}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in Y:\\
\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\langle\xi,\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\xi\rangle-CN^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6})\\
&\geq\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\subset\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}},\\
\dim(Y)=n\end{subarray}}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\xi\in Y:\\
\|\xi\|=1\end{subarray}}\langle\xi,\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\xi\rangle-CN^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6})\\
&=\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\lambda_{n}(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}})-CN^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6}).\end{split}

(3.8)

Here, we have abbreviated by
P_{\zeta}
the spectral projection of
\mathcal{J}_{N}-E_{N}
associated with the interval
(-\infty;\zeta]
. Up to the constant
\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
(and an error that vanishes as
N\to\infty
), we thus obtain a lower bound on the min-max values of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
through those of
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
.

Let us now diagonalize the quadratic operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
. We adapt here the arguments used in
[
15
]
for mean-field bosons. We recall the definitions of
H_{\text{GP}}
and
E
in (
1.6
) and of
\widetilde{\Phi}
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}
in (
2.49
), (
2.50
). We identify these operators with operators mapping
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
back into itself and, by slight abuse of notation, we still write
H_{\text{GP}},E,\widetilde{\Phi},\widetilde{\Gamma}
instead of
H_{\text{GP}}Q
,
EQ,Q\widetilde{\Phi}Q,Q\widetilde{\Gamma}Q
(recall that
Q=1-|\varphi_{0}\rangle\langle\varphi_{0}|
). Notice in particular that both
H_{\text{GP}}
and
E
are invertible in
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. In addition to these operators, we also define, on
L_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\begin{split}\widetilde{D}&=\widetilde{\Phi}-\widetilde{\Gamma}=e^{-\eta}H_{\text{GP}}\,e^{-\eta},\,\,\,\,\widetilde{E}=\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}(\widetilde{D}+2\widetilde{\Gamma})\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}\,\,\,\,\text{ with}\\
\widetilde{D}+2\widetilde{\Gamma}&=\widetilde{\Phi}+\widetilde{\Gamma}=e^{\eta}\big(H_{\text{GP}}+2N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)\big)e^{\eta}\end{split}

(3.9)

as well as

\begin{split}A={\widetilde{D}}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}^{-1/2},\hskip 14.22636ptB=\big(A^{-1}\big)^{*}=\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}^{1/2},\hskip 14.22636pt\alpha=\log\big(|A^{*}|\big).\end{split}

(3.10)

Using the polar decomposition, we also denote by
W
the partial isometry on
L_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
that is defined through

A=W|A|=|A^{*}|W,\hskip 14.22636ptB=|B^{*}|W.

(3.11)

The next lemma collects basic properties of the operators introduced above.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\widetilde{E},\widetilde{D}
be defined in
L_{\perp\varphi_{0}}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
by (
3.9
). Let
\ell\in(0;1)
be small enough and let
N
be large enough. Then:

a)

There exists
c>0
such that
\widetilde{E},\widetilde{D}\geq c>0
. In particular, the operators
A,B,\alpha
and
W
, defined in (
3.10
), (
3.11
) are well-defined in
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Moreover,
W
is unitary.

b)

There exist
c,C>0
such that
c\widetilde{D}^{2}\leq\widetilde{E}^{2}\leq C\widetilde{D}^{2}
. In particular,
c^{1/2}\widetilde{D}\leq\widetilde{E}\leq C^{1/2}\widetilde{D}
.

c)

There exists
C>0
such that
H_{\text{GP}}-C\leq\widetilde{D}\leq H_{\text{GP}}+C
.

d)

We have
\|A-1\|_{\text{HS}},\|B-1\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C
for some
C>0
.

e)

For every
\beta<1
, there exists
C>0
such that
\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\alpha\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C
. Moreover, there exists
C>0
such that
\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\alpha\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C
(from parts a), b),c), these estimates remain true if we replace factors of
\widetilde{D}
by the identity, by factors of
\widetilde{E}
or by factors of
H_{\text{GP}}
).

Proof.

We start with the proof of a). On
L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we have
H_{\text{GP}}\geq c>0
.Thus

\langle\psi,\widetilde{D}\psi\rangle=\langle e^{-\eta}\psi,H_{\text{GP}}e^{-\eta}\psi\rangle\geq c\|e^{-\eta}\psi\|^{2}\geq c(1-C\|\eta\|)\|\psi\|^{2}

for all
\psi\in L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. By Lemma
2.2
,
\|\eta\|\leq C\ell^{1/2}
. Thus
D\geq c>0
follows choosing
\ell>0
small enough. To prove the claim for
\widetilde{E}
, we compare first the operator with kernel
N^{3}V(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
with
8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}
. To this end, we observe that, for an arbitrary
\phi\in L^{2}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
,

\begin{split}&\left|\int dxdy\ \overline{\phi}(x)\phi(y)\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))-8\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\int dx\ |\phi(x)|^{2}\varphi_{0}(x)^{2}\right|\\
&\leq CN^{-1}\|\phi\|^{2}+\left|\int dxdy\ (Vf_{\ell})(y)\overline{\phi(x)}\varphi_{0}(x)\big(\phi(x+y/N)\varphi_{0}(x+y/N)-\phi(x)\varphi_{0}(x)\big)\right|\\
&\leq CN^{-1}\|\phi\|^{2}+CN^{-1}\|(-\Delta+1)^{1/2}\phi\|^{2}\leq CN^{-1}\|H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}\phi\|^{2}\end{split}

(3.12)

and thus

\begin{split}\langle\psi,(\widetilde{D}+2\widetilde{\Gamma})\psi\rangle&\geq\langle e^{\eta}\psi,\big(H_{\text{GP}}(1-C/N)+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2}\big)e^{\eta}\psi\rangle\\
&\geq\langle e^{\eta}\psi,H_{\text{GP}}(1-C/N)e^{\eta}\psi\rangle\geq c(1-C\|\eta\|)\|\psi\|^{2}\end{split}

for
N
large enough. This shows that
\widetilde{E}\geq c\widetilde{D}\geq c>0
. An immediate consequence is that
A,B,\alpha
and
W
as in (
3.10
), (
3.11
) are well-defined. Moreover,
W
is a unitary map, because
\text{ker}(W)=\text{ker}(A)=\{0\}
(recall that
\widetilde{E},\widetilde{D}\geq c>0
in
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
).

Let us switch to part b). We have

\widetilde{E}^{2}=\widetilde{D}^{1/2}(\widetilde{D}+2\widetilde{\Gamma})\widetilde{D}^{1/2}=\widetilde{D}^{2}+2\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{\Gamma}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}.

With (
3.6
) (and with part a)), we conclude that

\widetilde{E}^{2}\leq\widetilde{D}^{2}+C\widetilde{D}\leq C\widetilde{D}^{2}

and also that
\widetilde{E}^{2}\geq\widetilde{D}^{2}-C\widetilde{D}
, which implies (from the proof of a), we have
\widetilde{E}\geq c\widetilde{D}
)

\widetilde{D}^{2}\leq\widetilde{E}^{2}+C\widetilde{D}\leq\widetilde{E}^{2}+C\widetilde{E}\leq C\widetilde{E}^{2}.

As for part c), we observe that

\widetilde{D}=e^{\eta}H_{\text{GP}}e^{\eta}=H_{\text{GP}}+(e^{\eta}-1)H_{\text{GP}}+H_{\text{GP}}(e^{\eta}-1)+(e^{\eta}-1)H_{\text{GP}}(e^{\eta}-1)

and that, from Lemma
2.2
,
(e^{\eta}-1)H_{\text{GP}}+H_{\text{GP}}(e^{\eta}-1)
and
(e^{\eta}-1)H_{\text{GP}}(e^{\eta}-1)
are bounded operators (the kernel of
k(-\Delta)+(-\Delta)k
has the form
(\Delta_{x}+\Delta_{y})(Nw_{\ell}(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
and can be handled as in (
3.7
), since the term
N^{3}(Vf_{\ell})(N(x-y))\varphi_{0}(x)\varphi_{0}(y)
corresponds to a bounded operator).

Let us now prove part d). We focus on the operator
A-1
. Using functional calculus (as in
[
15
, Lemma 3]
) we write

A-1=(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}-\widetilde{E}^{1/2})\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}=2\sqrt{2}\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{1/4}\frac{\widetilde{D}^{1/2}}{t+\widetilde{D}^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\frac{\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}}{t+\widetilde{E}^{2}}.

With (
3.6
) and with the bounds
\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}/(t+\widetilde{D}^{2})\|_{\text{op}}\leq C(t+1)^{-3/4}
,
\|(t+\widetilde{E}^{2})^{-1}\|_{\text{op}}\leq C(t+1)^{-1}
,
\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\|_{\text{op}}\leq C
(by part b), we obtain

\|A-1\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{1/4}\Big\|\frac{\widetilde{D}^{1/2}}{t+\widetilde{D}^{2}}\Big\|_{\text{op}}\big\|\widetilde{\Gamma}\big\|_{\text{HS}}\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\|_{\text{op}}\Big\|\frac{1}{t+\widetilde{E}^{2}}\Big\|\leq C\,.

Finally, let us prove part e). From part b), we have
0<c\leq AA^{*}\leq C
. For
x\in[c;C]
, we can bound

x-1-C(x-1)^{2}\leq\log x\leq x-1+C(1-x)^{2}.

Thus

\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\alpha\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}+C\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})^{2}\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}.

(3.13)

With functional calculus, we find

\begin{split}\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}&=\widetilde{D}^{(\beta+1)/2}(\widetilde{D}^{-1}-\widetilde{E}^{-1})\widetilde{D}^{(\beta+1)/2}\\
&=C\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{t^{1/2}}\frac{\widetilde{D}^{1+\beta/2}}{t+\widetilde{D}^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}D^{1/2}\frac{1}{t+\widetilde{E}^{2}}\widetilde{D}^{(\beta+1)/2}.\end{split}

Thus, with (
3.6
),
\|\widetilde{D}^{1+\beta/2}/(t+\widetilde{D}^{2})\|_{\text{op}},\|\widetilde{E}^{1+\beta/2}/(t+\widetilde{E}^{2})\|_{\text{op}}\leq C(t+1)^{-1/2+\beta/4}
and
\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\|_{\text{op}},\|\widetilde{E}^{-(\beta+1)/2}\widetilde{D}^{(\beta+1)/2}\|_{\text{op}}\leq C
(by part b)), we find

\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}dt\,t^{-1/2}(1+t)^{-1+\beta/2}\leq C

for any
\beta<1
. Since
\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})^{2}\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}(1-AA^{*})\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|^{2}_{\text{HS}}\leq C
, it follows from (
3.13
) that
\|\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\alpha\widetilde{D}^{\beta/2}\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C
, for all
\beta<1
. The bound for
\|\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\alpha\|_{\text{HS}}
can be proven similarly.
∎

Using the operators defined above, we can now construct a suitable Bogoliubov transformation that diagonalizes
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
. To this end, we denote by
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
the eigenbasis of
\widetilde{E}
, we set
a^{\sharp}_{j}=a^{\sharp}(\varphi_{j})
for
\sharp\in\{\cdot,*\}
,
\alpha_{ij}=\langle\varphi_{i},\alpha\,\varphi_{j}\rangle
and we define

\mathcal{W}=\Gamma(W),\hskip 14.22636ptX=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{ij}a^{*}_{i}a^{*}_{j}-\text{h.c.},\hskip 14.22636pt\mathcal{U}=e^{X}\mathcal{W}.

(3.14)

Note in particular that
\mathcal{U}:\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\to\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
and recall that
\mathcal{U}
acts as

\begin{split}&\mathcal{U}^{*}a^{*}(f)\mathcal{U}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}a^{*}(W^{*}|A^{*}|f)+\frac{1}{2}a^{*}(W^{*}|A^{*}|^{-1}f)+\frac{1}{2}a(W^{*}|A^{*}|\bar{f})-\frac{1}{2}a^{*}(W^{*}|A^{*}|^{-1}\bar{f})\\
&=\frac{1}{2}a^{*}(\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}f)+\frac{1}{2}a^{*}(\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}f)+\frac{1}{2}a(\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\bar{f})-\frac{1}{2}a(\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\bar{f})\end{split}

for
f\in L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, by the definitions in (
3.10
). From
[
15
]
, we recall that
\mathcal{U}
is constructed so that
\mathcal{U}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\mathcal{U}
is diagonal. Indeed, representing
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
as

\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\langle\varphi_{i},\widetilde{\Phi}\,\varphi_{j}\rangle\,a^{*}_{i}a_{j}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\langle\varphi_{i},\widetilde{\Gamma}\,\varphi_{j}\rangle\big(a^{*}_{i}a^{*}_{j}+\text{h.c.}\big),

a lengthy, but straightforward calculation verifies that

\begin{split}\mathcal{U}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\mathcal{U}&=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2})-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{ij}+\widetilde{\Gamma}_{ij}\big)a^{*}(\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\varphi_{i})a(\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\varphi_{j})\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{i,j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}\big(\widetilde{\Phi}_{ij}-\widetilde{\Gamma}_{ij}\big)a^{*}(\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\varphi_{i})a(\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\varphi_{j})\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\big(\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2})-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\big)+d\Gamma(\widetilde{E}),\end{split}

(3.15)

where
\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
denotes the trace in
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. As a consequence of (
3.8
), we obtain that if
\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})-E_{N}\leq\zeta
, then

\begin{split}\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})\geq\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}&+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\right)-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)\\
&+\lambda_{n}\big(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})\big)-CN^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6}).\end{split}

(3.16)

To obtain a lower bound for the eigenvalues of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
(and hence of
H_{N}
) matching the statement of Theorem

1.1
, we still have to compare the operator
\widetilde{E}
, defined in (
3.9
), with the operator
E
, defined in (
1.6
). This is done in the next lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

On
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
we have that

\begin{split}&(1-C/N)\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}\leq\widetilde{E}\\
&\hskip 170.71652pt\leq(1+C/N)\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}\end{split}

for a constant
C>0
, independent of
N
. Moreover, the operator
E
defined in (
1.6
) is unitarily equivalent to
\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}
. We conclude that

(1-C/N)\lambda_{n}(E)\leq\lambda_{n}(\widetilde{E})\leq(1+C/N)\lambda_{n}(E).

Proof.

Using the bound (
3.12
), we see that

(1-C/N)e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\leq(\widetilde{D}+2\widetilde{\Gamma})\leq(1+C/N)e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta},

which immediately implies the first claim. The statement about the unitary equivalence of
\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}
and
E
follows if we write

\widetilde{D}^{1/2}=|H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}e^{-\eta}|=U_{0}^{*}H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}e^{-\eta}=e^{-\eta}H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}U_{0}

for some partial isometry
U_{0}
, by polar decomposition. Since
e^{-\eta}
is bijective and
H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}
is injective, we see that
\text{ker}\big(H_{\text{GP}}^{1/2}e^{-\eta}\big)=\{0\}
and hence
U_{0}
is a unitary map. This implies that

\big(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}e^{\eta}(H_{\text{GP}}+16\pi\mathfrak{a}_{0}\varphi_{0}^{2})e^{\eta}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\big)^{1/2}=U_{0}^{*}EU_{0}
.
∎

With Lemma
3.2
and Eq. (
3.16
), we conclude for
\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})-E_{N}\leq\zeta
that

\begin{split}\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})&\geq\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\right)-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\lambda_{n}\big(d\Gamma(E)\big)-CN^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6})\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt-CN^{-1}\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\right)-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\right]\\
&\hskip 71.13188pt+\lambda_{n}(\mathcal{J}_{N})-\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}\Big).\end{split}

(3.17)

In the next two sections, we will prove that the ground state energy of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
is equal to
E_{N}=\kappa_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}+\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{2}(\widetilde{D}^{1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}+\widetilde{D}^{-1/2}\widetilde{E}\widetilde{D}^{1/2})-\widetilde{D}-\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)+\mathcal{O}\big(N^{-\rho}(1+\zeta^{6})\big)
. This will imply that the last term on the right hand side in (
3.17
) is a negligible error of order
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1}\zeta)
. The bound (
3.17
) then also shows that the spectrum of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
above
E_{N}
is bounded from below by that of
d\Gamma(E)
, up to errors that vanish in the limit
N\to\infty
. Note that the eigenvalues of
d\Gamma(E)
are explicitly given by finite sums of the form

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}n_{j}e_{j},

where the
(e_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
denote the eigenvalues of the operator
E
in
L^{2}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, defined in (
1.6
), and
n_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
are non-zero for finitely many
j\in\mathbb{N}
.

3.2
Upper Bound on
\sigma(H_{N})

In this section, we show that the spectrum of
d\Gamma(E)
provides an upper bound on the spectrum of
\mathcal{J}_{N}
above its ground state energy. To show this, we construct suitable trial states that approximately diagonalize
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
, defined in (
3.2
), and we apply Lemma
3.2
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}_{\mathcal{J}_{N}}
is defined in the full excitation space
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
, we can not directly choose its eigenvectors as trial states, but have to truncate them first to
\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}^{\leq N}
. Furthermore, in contrast to the lower bound, we also have to evaluate the potential energy
\mathcal{V}_{N}
of the trial states and show that it is negligible in the limit
N\to\infty
.

We start the analysis with some preliminary results on the conjugation of
\mathcal{N}
,
\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{N}
by the unitary map
\mathcal{U}
, defined in (
3.14
).

Lemma 3.3
.

For every
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a constant
C>0
such that in
\mathcal{F}_{\perp\varphi_{0}}
we have

\mathcal{U}^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{U}\leq C(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}.

(3.18)

Moreover, with
\widetilde{E}
as defined in (
3.9
), we have that

\mathcal{U}^{*}d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{U}\leq C(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})+\mathcal{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}

(3.19)

and consequently that

\mathcal{U}^{*}(\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{U}\leq C(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})+\mathcal{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}.

(3.20)

Proof.

Proceeding as in
[
10
, Lemma 3.1]
and using that
\|\alpha\|_{\text{HS}}\leq C
, we readily find (recalling the notation introduced in (
3.14
)), that

\sup_{s\in[-1;1]}\,e^{-sX}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}e^{sX}\leq C(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}.

The bound (
3.18
) then follows from
\mathcal{N}=d\Gamma(1)
so that
\mathcal{W}^{*}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{W}=(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}
.

Next, we note that, by (
3.19
) and Lemma
3.1
(part c)), we have

\mathcal{U}^{*}(\mathcal{K}+\mathcal{V}_{\text{ext}})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{U}\leq C\,(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}.

Hence, (
3.20
) is a consequence of (
3.19
). To prove the latter, we expand for
t\in[-1;1]

e^{-tX}d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}e^{tX}=d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}+\int_{0}^{t}ds\,e^{-sX}[d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j},X]e^{sX}.

Denoting (as we did around (
3.14
)) by
(\varphi_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
the eigenbasis of
\widetilde{E}
and setting
a^{\sharp}_{k}=a^{\sharp}(\varphi_{k})
, we find

[d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j},X]=\bigg(\sum_{k,l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}(\widetilde{E})\alpha_{kl}a^{*}_{k}a^{*}_{l}+\text{h.c.}\bigg)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}+d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})[(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j},X].

An application of Lemma
3.1
(part e)) and Cauchy-Schwarz shows that

\begin{split}\sum_{k,l=1}^{\infty}\lambda_{k}(\widetilde{E})\big|\alpha_{kl}\langle\xi,a^{*}_{k}a^{*}_{l}(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\big)\xi\rangle\big|&\leq\langle\xi,\big(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})+\|\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\alpha\|_{\text{HS}}^{2}\,(\mathcal{N}+1)\big)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\xi\rangle\\
&\leq\langle\xi,d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\xi\rangle+C\langle\xi,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j+1}\xi\rangle\end{split}

for all
\xi\in\mathcal{F}_{\bot\varphi_{0}}
. Moreover, observing that

[(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j},X]=\sum_{k,l=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{kl}a^{*}_{k}a^{*}_{l}\,j\,(\mathcal{N}+\Theta(\mathcal{N})+1)^{j-1}+\text{h.c.}

for some
\Theta:\mathbb{N}\to(0;1)
by the mean value theorem, we conclude similarly that

|\langle\xi,d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})[(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j},X]\xi\rangle|\leq C\langle\xi,d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\xi\rangle+C\langle\xi,(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j+1}\xi\rangle.

Applying Gronwall to the map
t\mapsto e^{-tX}d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}e^{tX}
and using (
3.18
), we find

\begin{split}\sup_{s\in[-1,1]}e^{-sX}d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}e^{sX}&\leq C(d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})+\mathcal{N}+1)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}.\end{split}

(3.21)

To conclude the bound (
3.19
), we then notice that

\mathcal{W}^{*}d\Gamma(\widetilde{E})(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}\mathcal{W}=d\Gamma(W^{*}\widetilde{E}\,W)(\mathcal{N}+1)^{j}

(3.22)

and that
W^{*}\widetilde{E}W\leq C\widetilde{E}
. From (
3.11
) and using Lemma
3.1
, part b), this last bound follows if we can show that

\Big\|\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}\widetilde{D}\widetilde{E}^{-1/2}}}\widetilde{E}^{1/2}\Big\|_{\text{op}}\leq C

(3.23)

uniformly in
N
. To prove (
